Unauthenticated inbound SFTP to goshs followed by remote file operations AIActions on Objectives Medium
Correlates an inbound connection accepted by a goshs process from a public IP with file create/modify/delete/rename events performed by that same goshs process shortly after — the observable ground truth of the CVE-2026-62325 bypass, where a remote party reads/writes/deletes files over SFTP with no authentication. Rationale: Anchors on the advisory's impact statement ('Unauthenticated SFTP file access (read, write, delete, rename)') by tying an inbound public connection accepted by the goshs process (default port 2121) to file create/modify/delete/rename by that same goshs process within 5 minutes — the exploitation footprint, since goshs itself emits no SSH auth log to prove the bypass. Cross-checked against: • https://advisories.gitlab.com/golang/github.com/patrickhener/goshs/v2/CVE-2026-62325/ • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-40884/
ATT&CKT1190 T1021.004 T1133
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate authenticated goshs SFTP use where the operator did configure proper credentials (the connection was authorized)
  • Internal file drops to a goshs share from a jump host with a public-routable but sanctioned IP
  • Backup or sync jobs writing to the goshs directory concurrently with an unrelated inbound connection
  • Vulnerability scanners connecting to port 2121 that coincide with unrelated file activity
Suppression filter
| where RemoteIP !in ("127.0.0.1") and not (InitiatingProcessCommandLine has "-fkf")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the RemoteIP and whether it is external/unexpected, and confirm the host's goshs was launched with an empty-credential -b value (pivot to the process-creation UCs). 2) Contain: kill goshs, block the source IP and the SFTP listener port, and snapshot the served directory to preserve evidence of read/write/delete. 3) Hunt: enumerate all file operations by goshs in the window to scope what was exfiltrated or tampered, and search for the same RemoteIP touching other exposed services.

Anomalous Host header on Poweradmin OIDC/SAML/logout endpoints (CVE-2026-54588) AIExploitation Medium
Hunts CVE-2026-54588 exploitation: requests to Poweradmin's /oidc/callback, SAML ACS/SLO, or /logout endpoints carrying an HTTP Host header value that has never been seen for that site in the 30-day baseline. Poweradmin embeds HTTP_HOST verbatim into the redirect_uri/ACS URL sent to the IdP, so an attacker-controlled Host value is the exploitation primitive. Rationale: Uses the article's exact vulnerable endpoints (/oidc/callback, SAML ACS/SLO, LogoutController) and the root-cause primitive (untrusted HTTP_HOST embedded in callback URLs). Baseline anti-join surfaces the attacker's poisoned Host (e.g. attacker.com) without hardcoding the victim's legit hostname. Cross-checked GHSA-3735-5339-xfwx and SentinelOne/NVD (CWE-20/601, CVSS 9.6). Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/poweradmin/poweradmin/security/advisories/GHSA-3735-5339-xfwx • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-54588/ • https://nvd.nist.gov/vuln/detail/CVE-2026-54588
ATT&CKT1190 T1656
Data sourcesWeb.Web
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate new vhost/hostname added to the Poweradmin front-end (rename, new DNS name, migration)
  • Uptime/health-check probes (Pingdom, StatusCake) that send synthetic Host headers
  • Load-balancer or Kubernetes ingress health probes hitting endpoints with pod-IP Host values
  • Vulnerability scanners run by the org's own security team (Nessus/Burp) sending crafted Host headers
  • CDN/WAF origin pulls that rewrite the Host to an internal origin name
Suppression filter
| where CsHost !in~ ("localhost","127.0.0.1") and CsHost !endswith ".internal" and CsHost !endswith ".svc.cluster.local" and not (CsUserAgent has_any ("Pingdom","StatusCake","kube-probe","ELB-HealthChecker"))
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull all requests to the anomalous CsHost on the auth endpoints; confirm the Host value is not an owned domain and check whether the request produced a 301/302 to an IdP authorize URL (redirect_uri would contain the attacker host). 2) Contain: set interface.base_url in Poweradmin config/settings.php (activates the safe branch immediately) and upgrade to 4.2.4/4.3.3; fix the reverse proxy so it does not pass proxy_set_header Host $http_host. 3) Hunt: check the IdP for AADSTS50011 redirect_uri-mismatch events and any successful admin sign-ins in the hour that followed; audit PowerDNS zones (MX, SPF/DKIM, CAA, delegation) for unauthorized changes.

| order by FirstSeen desc

Host header fuzzing burst against Poweradmin auth endpoints from single source AIReconnaissance Medium
Detects unauthenticated reconnaissance/injection probing for CVE-2026-54588: a single source IP hitting Poweradmin OIDC/SAML/logout endpoints with 2+ distinct HTTP Host header values inside a 10-minute window. This is the shape of an attacker enumerating which flow reflects HTTP_HOST into the outbound redirect_uri. Rationale: Self-contained fan-out on the article's specific auth endpoints; multiple distinct Host headers from one IP is the unambiguous signature of host-header injection probing described in the advisory (unauthenticated, no credentials required). No allowlist/placeholder needed. Thresholds (2 hosts, 3 requests) are conservative and self-evident from the attack shape. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/poweradmin/poweradmin/security/advisories/GHSA-3735-5339-xfwx • https://www.sentinelone.com/vulnerability-database/cve-2026-54588/

Spike of AADSTS50011 redirect_uri mismatch at IdP (poisoned Poweradmin callback) AIExploitation Medium
Detects the IdP-side footprint of CVE-2026-54588 when the OIDC/SAML IdP is Entra ID: a burst of AADSTS50011 (redirect_uri / reply-URL mismatch) failures on a single application within an hour. Entra rejects the attacker-poisoned redirect_uri (attacker.com/oidc/callback) because it is not a registered reply URL, so a cluster of 50011 on the Poweradmin app is high-fidelity evidence of active redirect_uri poisoning. Rationale: Directly maps the article's exploitation mechanic (poisoned redirect_uri sent to the IdP) to the IdP error it produces. Corroborated by Microsoft Learn / SSOJet: AADSTS50011 'spiking on URIs you never deployed' is the documented signature of an attacker probing crafted redirect_uri values — exactly this CVE. 5/hr threshold separates probing from a single stray config typo. Cross-checked against: • https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/troubleshoot/entra/entra-id/app-integration/error-code-aadsts50011-redirect-uri-mismatch • https://github.com/poweradmin/poweradmin/security/advisories/GHSA-3735-5339-xfwx
ATT&CKT1528 T1190
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine app-registration reply-URL misconfiguration after a Poweradmin upgrade or hostname change
  • A single trailing-slash / http-vs-https typo in the app's configured base_url producing repeated 50011 for legit users
  • Developer/staging instance of Poweradmin pointed at the production app registration
  • Reply-URL propagation lag (~60s) right after an admin edits the app registration
  • Load-balanced deployment where one node has a stale/incorrect base_url
Suppression filter
| where not (SrcIPs has_any ("10.","192.168.") and Users == 1)
Response runbook
1) Triage: inspect the failing redirect_uri/reply-URL values in the sign-in detail — legit misconfig repeats one owned URL, an attack shows external/attacker hostnames or many distinct URIs. 2) Contain: if attacker hosts are present, treat affected users as at-risk (revoke sessions/refresh tokens for any who authenticated), lock the app to strict reply-URL matching, and force Poweradmin's interface.base_url + patch. 3) Hunt: pivot to successful sign-ins for the same users from new IPs in the following hour (see companion UC), and audit PowerDNS zones for MX/SPF/DKIM/CAA tampering.
AADSignInEventsBeta
| where Timestamp > ago(7d)
| where ErrorCode == 50011   // AADSTS50011 redirect_uri / reply-URL mismatch
| summarize AttemptCount=count(), Users=dcount(AccountUpn), UserList=make_set(AccountUpn,20), SrcIPs=make_set(IPAddress,20), FirstSeen=min(Timestamp), LastSeen=max(Timestamp) by Application, ApplicationId, bin(Timestamp, 1h)
| where AttemptCount >= 5   // 5+ reply-URL mismatches/hr on one app = probing, not a one-off config typo
| order by AttemptCount desc
SigninLogs
| where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
| where ResultType == "50011"
| summarize AttemptCount=count(), Users=dcount(UserPrincipalName), UserList=make_set(UserPrincipalName,20), SrcIPs=make_set(IPAddress,20), FirstSeen=min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen=max(TimeGenerated) by AppDisplayName, AppId, bin(TimeGenerated, 1h)
| where AttemptCount >= 5
| order by AttemptCount desc

Victim account takeover: new-IP sign-in within 1h of redirect_uri mismatch (CVE-2026-54588) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Correlates the two halves of a successful CVE-2026-54588 account takeover on Entra: a successful sign-in for a user from an IP absent from their 90-day baseline, occurring within one hour of an AADSTS50011 redirect_uri-mismatch event for that same user. Catches the case where the attacker captured the victim's authorization code via a poisoned redirect_uri and then used it/logged in. Rationale: Implements reviewer suggestion #1 and ties the article's stated outcome ('IdP redirects the victim's authorization code to an attacker' → full account takeover, no credentials) to observable telemetry: a fresh-IP success closely following a 50011 for the same user. Baseline anti-join + 1h temporal window is high-fidelity and article-specific to the redirect-poisoning flow. Cross-checked against: • https://github.com/poweradmin/poweradmin/security/advisories/GHSA-3735-5339-xfwx • https://learn.microsoft.com/en-us/troubleshoot/entra/entra-id/app-integration/error-code-aadsts50011-redirect-uri-mismatch
ATT&CKT1528 T1550.001
Data sourcesAuthentication.Authentication
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • User travels / switches to a new mobile-carrier or home IP the same hour a legit 50011 config error fired
  • VPN or corporate-proxy egress-IP rotation introducing a 'new' IP for an existing user
  • New device enrollment where the user re-auths from an unfamiliar network after an app-registration reply-URL fix
  • Roaming user behind CGNAT whose apparent IP changes between the failure and the success
  • Admin testing the app registration (deliberately triggering 50011) then logging in from a lab network
Suppression filter
| where DelayMin <= 60 and NewIP !startswith "10." and NewIP !startswith "192.168."
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the success IP's geo/ASN vs the user's baseline and whether the 50011 FailIP differs; check what the session did next (consent grants, PowerDNS zone edits, mail-flow rules). 2) Contain: revoke the user's sessions and refresh tokens, force password reset + re-MFA, and block the new IP; if the user is a Poweradmin admin, freeze PowerDNS zone changes. 3) Hunt: review PowerDNS for MX hijack, SPF/DKIM additions, CAA removals and subdomain delegation; sweep other users for the same 50011→new-IP pattern and confirm the app is patched (4.2.4/4.3.3) with interface.base_url set.
let Lookback = 7d;
let WindowHours = 1h;
let BaselineDays = 90d;
let UserIpBaseline = AADSignInEventsBeta
    | where Timestamp between (ago(BaselineDays) .. ago(Lookback))
    | where ErrorCode == 0
    | summarize by AccountUpn, IPAddress;
let RedirectFailures = AADSignInEventsBeta
    | where Timestamp > ago(Lookback)
    | where ErrorCode == 50011
    | project FailTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, FailIP = IPAddress, Application;
AADSignInEventsBeta
| where Timestamp > ago(Lookback)
| where ErrorCode == 0
| join kind=leftanti UserIpBaseline on AccountUpn, IPAddress
| join kind=inner RedirectFailures on AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (FailTime .. FailTime + WindowHours)
| project SuccessTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, NewIP = IPAddress, Country, Application, FailTime, FailIP, DelayMin = datetime_diff('minute', Timestamp, FailTime)
| order by SuccessTime desc

| order by SuccessTime desc

    | tstats summariesonly=true count as fail_count values(Authentication.src) as fail_src from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.action=failure Authentication.signature_id=50011 by Authentication.user
    | `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)` ]
| table firstTime user src app count fail_count fail_src
| sort - firstTime
| tstats summariesonly=false count min(_time) as firstTime from datamodel=Authentication where Authentication.action=success by Authentication.user Authentication.src Authentication.app _time span=1h
| `drop_dm_object_name(Authentication)`

IR Trends Q2 2026: Phishing and weaponized remote management tools drive attack chains

Cisco Talos
2026-07-28 23 use cases 41 techniques 7 kill-chain phases detected

Talos IR's Q2 report highlights a significant surge in phishing-based initial access and the weaponization of legitimate remote management tools. Learn how to sharpen your defenses.

Actions on Objectives
Detected
Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → [LLM] QR-code PDF phishing attachment resolving to workers.dev / pamconj M365 harvesting page (UAT-11764) · Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → [LLM] OAuth device-code authorization flow sign-in (ARToken/EvilTokens MFA bypass) · Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Trusted vendor binary / installer launching unusual children · Article-specific behavioural hunt — IR Trends Q2 2026: Phishing and weaponized remote management tools drive attack
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] Trojanized MeshAgent installed as SYSTEM auto-start service (Sinobi covert C2) · [LLM] Zoho Assist Unattended Agent deployment for headless remote access (Warlock / Storm-2603) · Scheduled task created with suspicious image / encoded args · RMM tool installed by non-IT user — remote-access utility for hands-on-keyboard
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] Endpoint egress/DNS to UAT-11764 / ARToken IOC infrastructure · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage. → [LLM] Post-compromise M365 defense-evasion inbox rule creation (UAT-11764 mailbox takeover) · [LLM] rclone data-exfiltration staging prior to Sinobi encryption · [LLM] Mass .SINOBI file-extension writes indicating domain-wide encryption · Remote service execution — PsExec / SMB lateral movement · MFA fatigue / push-bombing · OAuth consent / suspicious app grant · Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change · LSASS process access / dump (credential theft)
QR-code PDF phishing attachment resolving to workers.dev / pamconj M365 harvesting page (UAT-11764) AIDelivery High
Hunts inbound emails carrying an auto-generated PDF attachment whose extracted URL resolves to the UAT-11764 credential-harvesting infrastructure (clear90489058903-document.workers.dev or *.pamconj.com) rather than a legitimate M365 host. Article-specific because it keys the generic 'PDF with QR code' pattern to the concrete harvesting domains and the compromised-M365 propagation described by Talos. Rationale: Uses the pre-extracted harvesting IOCs (clear90489058903-document.workers.dev, *.pamconj.com) plus the article's explicit 'PDF attachment containing QR code -> M365 credential page' delivery, anchored on the PDF attachment + extracted-URL join so it only fires on the described lure, not any inbound PDF. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.001
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate vendor invoices/newsletters that happen to host content on *.workers.dev
  • Internal test phishing/awareness campaigns using workers.dev landing pages
  • A genuine SaaS product whose links live on Cloudflare Workers domains
  • PDF newsletters with tracking-redirect URLs that terminate on a workers.dev shortener
Suppression filter
| where SenderMailFromDomain !in~ ("trusted-vendor1.com","trusted-vendor2.com")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the PDF (PdfSHA256), detonate it, and confirm the QR/embedded URL points at the harvesting host; check whether the recipient clicked (UrlClickEvents) and authenticated. 2) Contain: quarantine all messages sharing the NetworkMessageId/subject cluster org-wide, block the harvesting domains at proxy/DNS, and force password + session-token revocation for any recipient who reached the page. 3) Hunt: search for the same sender/attachment hash across mailboxes, look for new inbox rules and anomalous SharePoint uploads on clicked accounts (see UC2), and pivot on internal senders (compromised mailboxes re-sending the lure).
let LookbackDays = 14d;
let HarvestDomains = dynamic(["clear90489058903-document.workers.dev","dashboard-bl.pamconj.com","spx.pamconj.com"]);
EmailEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EmailDirection == "Inbound" and DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
| join kind=inner (
    EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where FileType =~ "pdf" or FileName endswith ".pdf"
    | project NetworkMessageId, PdfName = FileName, PdfSHA256 = SHA256, FileSize
  ) on NetworkMessageId
| join kind=inner (
    EmailUrlInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
  ) on NetworkMessageId
| where UrlDomain in~ (HarvestDomains) or UrlDomain endswith "pamconj.com" or UrlDomain endswith ".workers.dev"
| project Timestamp, SenderFromAddress, SenderMailFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress, Subject, PdfName, PdfSHA256, Url, UrlDomain
| order by Timestamp desc

Post-compromise M365 defense-evasion inbox rule creation (UAT-11764 mailbox takeover) AIActions on Objectives Medium
Detects creation/modification of Exchange Online inbox rules that delete, move-to-junk, mark-as-read, or forward mail immediately after an account is phished — the exact defense-evasion behaviour Talos attributes to UAT-11764 for hiding attacker replies while the campaign propagates via the mailbox's contacts. Rationale: Directly implements the article's 'creating email inbox rules for defense evasion' post-compromise action, scoped to the evasion-signalling rule parameters (delete/move/hide/forward) so it does not fire on benign organizational-signature or vacation rules. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/
ATT&CKT1564.008 T1114.003
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • User legitimately creates a 'move newsletters to folder' rule
  • Helpdesk configuring a shared-mailbox auto-forward for coverage
  • Executive assistant setting delegate forwarding rules
  • Third-party mail add-in (e.g. CRM) programmatically creating filing rules
Suppression filter
| where IPAddress !in ("<corporate-egress-ips-if-known>") | where UserAgent !has "MailboxAssistant"
Response runbook
1) Triage: correlate the rule-creating AccountId/IPAddress against SigninLogs — was the sign-in from a new device/geo or a device-code flow? Read the raw rule to see what it hides/forwards. 2) Contain: delete the malicious rule, revoke sessions/refresh tokens, reset credentials, and enforce phishing-resistant MFA on the account. 3) Hunt: check the account for outbound phishing to internal contacts, SharePoint/OneDrive file staging, and OAuth grants; sweep for the same ClientIP/UserAgent creating rules on other mailboxes.
let LookbackDays = 14d;
CloudAppEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where ActionType in ("New-InboxRule","Set-InboxRule","UpdateInboxRules")
| extend Raw = tostring(RawEventData)
| where Raw has_any ("DeleteMessage","MoveToFolder","Junk Email","Deleted Items","MarkAsRead","ForwardTo","RedirectTo","ForwardAsAttachmentTo")
| project Timestamp, AccountDisplayName, AccountId, ActionType, IPAddress, CountryCode, ISP, UserAgent, Raw
| order by Timestamp desc

OfficeActivity

| order by TimeGenerated desc

OAuth device-code authorization flow sign-in (ARToken/EvilTokens MFA bypass) AIExploitation Medium
Surfaces successful Entra ID sign-ins completed via the OAuth device authorization flow — the exact MFA-bypass primitive the ARToken PhaaS platform exposes across its 80+ endpoints. Buckets by account to catch device-code fan-out and rare-geo approvals rather than every legitimate device onboarding. Rationale: Keys on ARToken's core capability — 'bypass MFA through the OAuth device authorization flow rather than stealing passwords' — via the deviceCodeFlow marker in AuthenticationProcessingDetails, aggregating per account to expose the multi-flow/rare-geo pattern of a phishing operator. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/
ATT&CKT1621 T1078.004
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Legitimate onboarding of IoT/kiosk/PowerShell CLI devices that use device-code auth
  • Conference-room / Teams Rooms device enrollment
  • Developers authenticating Azure CLI on headless build agents
  • Smart-TV or streaming apps signing into corporate accounts
Suppression filter
| where AccountUpn !in~ ("svc-devicecode-kiosk@contoso.com","teamsroom01@contoso.com")
Response runbook
1) Triage: for each flagged AccountUpn compare the device-code IPs/countries against the user's normal sign-in pattern and check what resource/app the token was minted for. 2) Contain: revoke refresh tokens and PRTs, disable the device-code grant via Conditional Access for that user group, and reset credentials if the geo/app is anomalous. 3) Hunt: pivot on the same IPs to other accounts, look for immediate mailbox/SharePoint access, new inbox rules (UC2), and PRT-based non-interactive sign-ins following the device-code event.
let LookbackDays = 14d;
AADSignInEventsBeta

| where ErrorCode == 0
| where tostring(AuthenticationProcessingDetails) has "deviceCodeFlow" or tostring(AuthenticationProcessingDetails) has "Device Code"
| summarize SignIns = count(),
            Apps = make_set(Application, 10),
            IPs = make_set(IPAddress, 15),
            Countries = make_set(Country, 10),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp)
  by AccountUpn
| order by SignIns desc

Trojanized MeshAgent installed as SYSTEM auto-start service (Sinobi covert C2) AIInstallation High
Detects a MeshAgent/MeshCentral binary being launched by services.exe under SYSTEM — the covert durable backdoor Sinobi operators used as their primary C2 for ~3 days before encryption. Focuses on the service-install persistence rather than any legitimate interactive MeshCentral use. Rationale: Implements the article's explicit Sinobi TTP: 'trojanized MeshAgent installed as a SYSTEM-level auto-start service.' The services.exe parent + SYSTEM context is the differentiator that separates the persistence install from a legitimate MeshCentral console session. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/ • https://www.microsoft.com/en-us/wdsi/threats/malware-encyclopedia-description?Name=Backdoor%3AWin64%2FMeshAgent.MTB
ATT&CKT1543.003 T1219
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • An IT team that legitimately runs MeshCentral for remote support installs the agent as a service
  • MSP-managed endpoints where MeshAgent is a sanctioned RMM
  • Security-testing labs deploying MeshCentral for research
  • Reinstallation/upgrade of an approved MeshCentral fleet
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("<authorized-meshcentral-hosts>") and SHA256 !in ("<approved-meshagent-sha256>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm the SHA256 against your known-good MeshCentral hash and check whether this host is in an authorized MeshCentral deployment inventory; inspect the service's config/command line for the C2 host. 2) Contain: stop and disable the service, isolate the host, and block the resolved C2 endpoint (WSS/443) at the perimeter. 3) Hunt: pull DeviceNetworkEvents for MeshAgent WSS egress to non-corporate servers, check for RDP/WinRM lateral movement from this host, ntds.dit access, and stage the environment for imminent ransomware (see UC7/UC8).
let LookbackDays = 14d;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where FileName =~ "meshagent.exe" or FileName =~ "meshagent64.exe"
     or ProcessVersionInfoProductName has "MeshCentral"
     or ProcessVersionInfoOriginalFileName has "MeshAgent"
     or ProcessVersionInfoFileDescription has "Mesh Agent"
| where InitiatingProcessFileName =~ "services.exe"
| where AccountName in~ ("system","local system") or InitiatingProcessAccountName =~ "system"
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
          ProcessCommandLine, ProcessVersionInfoProductName,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessAccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
let LookbackDays = 14d;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID in (4697, 7045)
| where ServiceFileName has "meshagent" or ServiceName has "mesh" or ServiceFileName has "MeshCentral"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account = SubjectUserName, ServiceName, ServiceFileName, ServiceStartType, ServiceAccount
| order by TimeGenerated desc

  category: process_creation
  product: windows
detection:
  selection_img:
    - Image|endswith:
        - '\\meshagent.exe'
        - '\\meshagent64.exe'
    - Product|contains: 'MeshCentral'
    - OriginalFileName: 'MeshAgent.exe'
  selection_parent:
    ParentImage|endswith: '\\services.exe'
  condition: selection_img and selection_parent
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:1 @ParentImage:(*\\services.exe OR *\\Services.exe) @Image:(*\\MeshAgent.exe OR *\\meshagent.exe OR *\\MeshAgent64.exe OR *\\meshagent64.exe)
#event_simpleName=ProcessRollup2 ParentBaseFileName=/services\.exe$/i ImageFileName=/meshagent(64)?\.exe$/i
| groupBy([aid, ComputerName, ImageFileName, SHA256HashData], function=count())
Endpoint egress/DNS to UAT-11764 / ARToken IOC infrastructure AICommand & Control Medium
Alerts on any host resolving or connecting to the corroborated campaign IOC domains (dashboard-bl.pamconj.com, spx.pamconj.com, clear90489058903-document.workers.dev). A direct IOC-match tripwire for both the phishing-panel/C2 (pamconj) and QR-harvesting (workers.dev) infrastructure. Rationale: Direct match on the three pre-extracted campaign IOC domains for a low-cost tripwire. Confidence is Medium (not High) because these domains are cited only in the Talos article and were not independently confirmed by a second source during cross-checking — validate before treating a hit as definitive. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1102
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher/analyst deliberately browsing the phishing page from a corporate host during triage
  • Sandbox/detonation VM that egresses through corporate DNS
  • Threat-intel enrichment tooling actively resolving IOC domains
  • A future benign re-registration of the pamconj.com domain after takedown
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("<soc-triage-vm>","<sandbox-detonation-host>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating process/user; a browser hit suggests a clicked phish, a non-browser process suggests C2/automation. 2) Contain: sinkhole/block the domains at DNS and proxy, and if a user reached the harvesting page revoke their tokens and reset credentials. 3) Hunt: correlate the ClientIP/user to inbound QR-PDF emails (UC1), device-code sign-ins (UC3), and subsequent mailbox rule / SharePoint staging activity.

Zoho Assist Unattended Agent deployment for headless remote access (Warlock / Storm-2603) AIInstallation Medium
Detects installation/execution of the Zoho Assist Unattended Agent — the RMM tool Warlock (Storm-2603) deployed to gain administrative remote control of endpoints with no user logged in. Article-specific to the 'unattended' agent variant used for stealthy persistence rather than an interactive Zoho Assist support session. Rationale: Implements the article's Warlock/Storm-2603 observation of the 'Zoho Assist Unattended Agent' installer, keyed on the Zoho vendor metadata plus the 'Unattended'/service-install markers that distinguish the persistent headless variant from a one-off support session. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/ • https://cybersecuritynews.com/hackers-use-velociraptor-cloudflare-tunnels-zoho-assist/
ATT&CKT1219 T1543.003
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: medium — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Sanctioned IT/MSP fleet where Zoho Assist Unattended is the approved RMM
  • Helpdesk pushing Zoho Assist to a new endpoint for legitimate remote support
  • Software-deployment tooling reinstalling/upgrading Zoho Assist
  • Vendor support session that the user approved that installs the unattended agent
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("<authorized-zoho-assist-hosts>") and InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("<approved-deployment-tool>.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: verify whether Zoho Assist is a sanctioned RMM in this environment and whether this host/user is authorized; check the installing parent process and account. 2) Contain: if unauthorized, uninstall the agent, disable the ZohoURSService, isolate the host, and revoke any Zoho account access tied to the unattended enrollment. 3) Hunt: look for other RMM tools installed in the same window (MeshAgent — UC4), credential-store access (ntds.dit), and lateral RDP/WinRM originating from the host.
let LookbackDays = 14d;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where ProcessVersionInfoProductName has "Zoho Assist"
     or ProcessVersionInfoCompanyName has "ZOHO"
     or FileName has_any ("ZohoMeeting.exe","ZA_Access.exe","ZA_Connect.exe","ZMAgent.exe","ZohoURSService.exe")
     or ProcessCommandLine has "Zoho Assist"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("Unattended","install","-install","UnAttended","ZA_Access","urs")
     or FileName has_any ("ZA_Access.exe","ZohoURSService.exe","ZMAgent.exe")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc

| where (EventID == 4688 and NewProcessName has "Zoho" and (CommandLine has "Unattended" or CommandLine has "install" or NewProcessName has_any ("ZA_Access","ZohoURSService","ZMAgent")))
     or (EventID in (7045,4697) and (ServiceName has "Zoho" or ServiceFileName has "Zoho"))

rclone data-exfiltration staging prior to Sinobi encryption AIActions on Objectives High
Detects rclone.exe (including renamed copies, via OriginalFileName) executing cloud-transfer subcommands — the exfil-staging tool observed in the Sinobi engagement before domain-wide encryption. Pairs the binary identity with rclone-specific transfer flags to avoid firing on the mere presence of the tool. Rationale: Article names rclone.exe explicitly as the Sinobi exfil-staging tool. The OriginalFileName check catches renamed copies, and requiring transfer subcommands/flags keeps it high-fidelity rather than firing on the binary alone. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/ • https://www.picussecurity.com/threat-database/how-sinobi-ransomware-encrypts-files-and-destroys-backups
ATT&CKT1567.002 T1048
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Backup/sysadmin scripts that legitimately use rclone to sync to corporate cloud storage
  • DevOps CI pipeline pushing artifacts to S3/B2 via rclone
  • Power user syncing personal cloud storage (sanctioned)
  • Data-migration project using rclone for a bulk one-time transfer
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("veeam.backup.shell.exe","backup-runner.exe") and DeviceName !in ("<approved-backup-hosts>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: read the full command line to identify the remote target (cloud provider/bucket), source path, and volume; check whether the account/host normally runs rclone. 2) Contain: kill the process, block the destination endpoint, isolate the host, and preserve the rclone config file for scoping. 3) Hunt: this is a pre-encryption signal — immediately check for MeshAgent C2 (UC4), lateral RDP/WinRM, ntds.dit access, and any .SINOBI file writes (UC8) across the domain.
let LookbackDays = 14d;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where AccountName !endswith "$"
| where FileName =~ "rclone.exe" or ProcessVersionInfoOriginalFileName =~ "rclone.exe"
| where ProcessCommandLine has_any ("copy","sync","move","lsd","--config","--transfers","--no-check-certificate","--multi-thread-streams","--ignore-existing","mega:","b2:")
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine
| order by Timestamp desc
let LookbackDays = 14d;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where NewProcessName endswith "\\rclone.exe" or CommandLine has_any ("rclone","--transfers","--no-check-certificate","--multi-thread-streams")
| where CommandLine has_any ("copy","sync","move","--config")
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, SubjectUserName, NewProcessName, CommandLine, ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

Mass .SINOBI file-extension writes indicating domain-wide encryption AIActions on Objectives High
Detects a host writing a large number of files with the .SINOBI extension in a short window — the terminal impact stage of a Sinobi ransomware event deployed via GPO logon script across the domain. Thresholded per host so it fires on active encryption, not a single stray artifact. Rationale: Uses the concrete .SINOBI encryption extension named in the article as the impact indicator, thresholded at >25 distinct files per host to separate an active encryption run from an incidental artifact and to surface the encrypting process for immediate scoping. Cross-checked against: • https://blog.talosintelligence.com/ir-trends-q2-2026/ • https://www.picussecurity.com/threat-database/how-sinobi-ransomware-encrypts-files-and-destroys-backups
ATT&CKT1486
Data sources
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A file-naming convention or backup product that coincidentally appends .SINOBI (extremely rare)
  • Malware researcher generating .SINOBI samples in a sanctioned lab
  • Restore/replay of previously-encrypted files during recovery testing
  • Security team detonating a Sinobi sample on an isolated host that reports to the same tenant
Suppression filter
| where DeviceName !in ("<malware-analysis-lab-hosts>")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the encrypting process (Actor/ActorSHA256) and confirm scope via EncryptedFiles/FirstSeen; this is an active incident, treat as confirmed. 2) Contain: isolate the affected host(s) immediately, disable the malicious GPO logon script and block its distribution, and shut down SMB write paths to limit spread. 3) Hunt: pull the encrypting binary for IR, trace the GPO/logon-script origin, review MeshAgent C2 (UC4) and rclone exfil (UC7) that preceded encryption, and check every domain host for the same actor process to gauge blast radius.
let LookbackDays = 14d;
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileCreated","FileModified")
| where FileName endswith ".SINOBI"
| summarize EncryptedFiles = dcount(FolderPath),
            SampleFiles = make_set(FolderPath, 5),
            FirstSeen = min(Timestamp), LastSeen = max(Timestamp),
            Actor = any(InitiatingProcessFileName),
            ActorCmd = any(InitiatingProcessCommandLine),
            ActorSHA256 = any(InitiatingProcessSHA256)
  by DeviceName
| where EncryptedFiles > 25
| order by EncryptedFiles desc
let LookbackDays = 14d;
ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where TargetFileName endswith ".SINOBI" or TargetFilePath endswith ".SINOBI"
| summarize EncryptedFiles = dcount(TargetFilePath),
            FirstSeen = min(TimeGenerated), LastSeen = max(TimeGenerated),
            Actor = any(ActingProcessName), ActorCmd = any(ActingProcessCommandLine)
  by DvcHostname
| where EncryptedFiles > 25
| order by EncryptedFiles desc

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

| tstats `summariesonly` count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count
| tstats summariesonly=false count, values(All_Traffic.dest_port) AS ports
    from datamodel=Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
    where All_Traffic.action="allowed" AND All_Traffic.dest_category!="internal"
    by _time span=10s, All_Traffic.src, All_Traffic.dest
| `drop_dm_object_name(All_Traffic)`
| streamstats current=f last(_time) AS prev_time by src, dest
| eval delta = _time - prev_time
| stats avg(delta) AS avg_delta stdev(delta) AS sd_delta count by src, dest
| where count > 30 AND sd_delta < 5 AND avg_delta>=30 AND avg_delta<=600
| sort - count

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats summariesonly=false count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats summariesonly=false earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Attachment delivery + execution by recipient — most common malware initial access.
ATT&CKT1566.001 T1204.002
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

            by UserPrincipalName, bin(TimeGenerated, 5m)

| order by Attempts desc

Ransomware-style mass file rename / extension change Actions on Objectives Medium
Threshold detection: many files renamed in short window, often with new extension.
ATT&CKT1486
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem DeviceFileEvents
DeviceFileEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
| where ActionType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(FileName) by DeviceName, InitiatingProcessAccountName, bin(Timestamp, 1m)
| where files > 200    // empirical: > 200 unique-file renames in 1m by one account on one host
                       //            is well above the P99 of legitimate bulk-tooling
| order by files desc

ImFileEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(1d)
| where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
| where EventType in ("FileRenamed","FileModified")
| summarize files = dcount(TargetFileName)
            by DvcHostname, ActorUsername, ActingProcessName, bin(TimeGenerated, 1m)
| where files > 200    // P99 of legit bulk-tooling per-minute rename volume
| order by files desc
ENDPOINT
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
| tstats `summariesonly` count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files
| tstats summariesonly=false count, dc(Filesystem.file_name) AS files
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("modified","renamed")
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, _time span=1m
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
| where files > 200
| sort - files

                                          "lsm.exe","SearchProtocolHost.exe")

          InitiatingProcessFileName, InitiatingProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFolderPath, AccountName
| order by Timestamp desc
// SecurityEvent EventID 4663 = object access. AccessMask 0x10 / 0x40 / 0x1010

// Object Access > Audit File System policy required.
SecurityEvent

| where ObjectName endswith "\lsass.exe"
| where Account !endswith "$"

status: experimental
description: |

            - '\Program Files\'

Reconnaissance. Attacker researches the target — OSINT, scanning, enumeration.
Weaponization. Coupling exploit code with deliverable payload (e.g. weaponized PDF/Office doc, trojanized installer).
Delivery. Transmitting the weapon (phishing email, USB, watering hole, drive-by, malicious ad). → Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution · Email attachment opened from external sender
Exploitation. Triggering the exploit — vulnerability/macro/trust abuse to execute code. → Office app spawning script/LOLBin child process · Fake CAPTCHA / clipboard-injected PowerShell (ClickFix / FakeCaptcha) · Article-specific behavioural hunt — Mirage Kitten targets Middle East and Africa region with new malware
Installation. Establishing persistence on the host — backdoor, service, scheduled task, registry key. → [LLM] NightLedger DLL search-order hijack: SspiCli.dll side-load via AppVShNotify.exe · [LLM] BridgeHead WebSocket tunneler DLL drop (unbcl.dll / libwinpthread-1.dll in anomalous paths) · File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match
Command & Control. Beacon to attacker infrastructure for control and tasking. → [LLM] NightLedger C2 beacon to realhealthshop.com / tjconsultingservices.com · [LLM] BridgeHead WebSocket C2 to smartconnect.azurewebsites.net with hardcoded Edg/86 UA · [LLM] AppVShNotify.exe making outbound public network connections (NightLedger host beaconing) · Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations · Network connections to article IPs / domains
Actions on Objectives. Lateral movement, credential dumping, data theft, ransomware, sabotage.
NightLedger DLL search-order hijack: SspiCli.dll side-load via AppVShNotify.exe AIInstallation High
Hunts the NightLedger loader, which masquerades as SspiCli.dll and abuses delay-loaded RPCRT4 imports of the legitimate signed AppVShNotify.exe to gain execution. Fires when SspiCli.dll is loaded from any path outside the Windows system directories, where the genuine module never lives. Rationale: Anchored on the article's exact side-load pair (SspiCli.dll masquerade against AppVShNotify.exe). The genuine SspiCli.dll ships only in System32/SysWOW64/WinSxS, so any other load path is high-fidelity. Cross-checked against Check Point Research and Nextron Systems, which document the same DLL-sideloading infection chain for this actor. Cross-checked against: • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/fast-and-furious-nimbus-manticore-operations-during-the-iranian-conflict/ • https://www.nextron-systems.com/2026/06/01/detecting-nimbus-manticore-and-their-sideloading-infection-chains/ • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/nimbus-manticore-deploys-nightledger.html
ATT&CKT1574.001 T1036.004
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Third-party software that ships a private copy of SspiCli.dll in its install directory (rare but possible with older redistributables)
  • Application-virtualization test/staging deployments copying App-V binaries to non-standard folders
  • Software-packaging tools (e.g. App-V sequencer) that stage system DLLs to a working directory
  • Backup/restore utilities re-creating DLLs into shadow/temp paths
Suppression filter
| where not (InitiatingProcessVersionInfoCompanyName == "Microsoft Corporation" and FolderPath startswith @"C:\Program Files\Microsoft Application Virtualization")
Response runbook
1) Triage: pull the loaded DLL's SHA256/MD5 and signer; confirm it is unsigned or not Microsoft-signed and that the hosting process is AppVShNotify.exe. Check the folder for a co-located AppVShNotify.exe (attacker-planted). 2) Contain: isolate the host, kill the AppVShNotify.exe process tree, and quarantine the rogue SspiCli.dll. 3) Hunt: on the same host and peers, query for the mutex A8215357-F99A-44FE-BC65-D8F0434B0C03, outbound HTTPS to realhealthshop.com / tjconsultingservices.com, and reads of C:\Windows\debug\NetSetup.log.
DeviceImageLoadEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(30d)
| where FileName =~ "SspiCli.dll"
| where FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\System32" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64" and FolderPath !startswith @"C:\Windows\WinSxS"
| extend SideloadHost = InitiatingProcessFileName
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, SideloadHost, InitiatingProcessFolderPath, LoadedDll = FileName, LoadedDllPath = FolderPath, SHA256, MD5
| order by Timestamp desc
Event
| where TimeGenerated > ago(30d)
| where Source == "Microsoft-Windows-Sysmon" and EventID == 7
| where EventData has "SspiCli.dll"
| where EventData !has @"C:\Windows\System32" and EventData !has @"C:\Windows\SysWOW64" and EventData !has @"C:\Windows\WinSxS"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, RenderedDescription

NightLedger C2 beacon to realhealthshop.com / tjconsultingservices.com AICommand & Control High
Hunts NightLedger's HTTPS command channel — periodic GET beacons and POST result exfiltration to the actor's primary and fallback C2 domains. Any resolution or connection to these hostnames from the estate is a confirmed compromise indicator. Rationale: Uses the two hard C2 domains named verbatim in the article (realhealthshop.com primary, tjconsultingservices.com fallback), both corroborated by The Hacker News and TechNadu write-ups of the same Kaspersky reporting. Direct IOC match = near-zero FP. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/nimbus-manticore-deploys-nightledger.html • https://www.technadu.com/mirage-kittens-new-malware-toolkit-targets-aerospace-and-defense-across-middle-east-and-africa/632036/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1041
Data sourcesNetwork_Resolution.DNS Network_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Security researcher / sandbox detonation of the sample inside the estate
  • Threat-intel enrichment or DNS sinkhole appliances resolving the IOC domain for blocklisting
  • Web-proxy or NDR product pre-fetching the domain for categorization
  • URL-scanning email security appliance resolving the host from a report
Suppression filter
| where InitiatingProcessFileName !in~ ("MsSense.exe","SenseIR.exe","MsMpEng.exe")
Response runbook
1) Triage: identify the initiating process (expect AppVShNotify.exe or rundll32-hosted); confirm it is not a known scanner. Check for the /edfcvfgbhnjmkqwasderfgg GET and POSTs to /qasxcdfvgbhnmyuioplkhnj or /wsdefvvbnhyuijkplmbgfrtt in proxy logs. 2) Contain: block both domains at DNS/proxy, isolate the host. 3) Hunt: sweep the estate for the SspiCli.dll side-load, the mutex, and beacons to the smartconnect.azurewebsites.net tunneler FQDN.

description: Detects DNS resolution of NightLedger primary and fallback C2 domains used by Mirage Kitten / UNC1549.
references:
  - https://securelist.com/mirage-kitten-new-tools/120811/
author: SOC Detection Engineering
date: 2026/08/04
tags:
  - attack.t1071.001
  - attack.t1041

  category: dns
  product: windows
detection:
  selection:
    QueryName|endswith:
      - 'realhealthshop.com'
      - 'tjconsultingservices.com'
  condition: selection
level: high
source:windows.sysmon @EventID:3 @DestinationHostname:(realhealthshop.com OR *.realhealthshop.com OR tjconsultingservices.com OR *.tjconsultingservices.com)

BridgeHead WebSocket tunneler DLL drop (unbcl.dll / libwinpthread-1.dll in anomalous paths) AIInstallation High
Hunts deployment of the BridgeHead SOCKS5-over-WebSocket tunneler, which was observed staged as unbcl.dll under %LocalAppData%\Microsoft\VisualStudio and as libwinpthread-1.dll under C:\program files (x86)\univpn\promote. Also matches the known sample MD5. Rationale: Anchored on the two exact staging paths (%LocalAppData%\Microsoft\VisualStudio\unbcl.dll and univpn\promote\libwinpthread-1.dll) plus the article's sample MD5 c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac. libwinpthread-1.dll is a real MinGW runtime, so the path — not the name — is the discriminator, and unbcl.dll is a masqueraded name. Cross-checked against: • https://thehackernews.com/2026/07/nimbus-manticore-deploys-nightledger.html • https://malwaretips.com/threads/iran-linked-apt-mirage-kitten-has-been-spotted-using-a-new-malware-toolkit-kaspersky-identified-nightledger-backdoor-and-bridgehead-tunneler.142539/

Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • A genuine Visual Studio / vcpkg toolchain writing libwinpthread-1.dll under its own install tree (note: the univpn\promote path is NOT a real VS path)
  • Legitimate UniVPN client updating its own binaries (verify signer and exact promote subfolder)
  • Developer copying MinGW runtime DLLs into a VisualStudio working directory
  • Software-deployment tooling restaging redistributables
Suppression filter

Response runbook
1) Triage: confirm file hash against c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac and check the writing process; genuine libwinpthread lives in toolchain dirs, not univpn\promote or VisualStudio LocalAppData. 2) Contain: quarantine the DLL, isolate host, and capture the rundll32/host process that loads it. 3) Hunt: look for the outbound WebSocket to smartconnect.azurewebsites.net and the fixed 8-byte tunnel wire-format traffic; sweep peers for the same two paths.

BridgeHead WebSocket C2 to smartconnect.azurewebsites.net with hardcoded Edg/86 UA AICommand & Control High
Hunts the BridgeHead tunneler's HTTPS WebSocket upgrade (GET /connect) to smartconnect.azurewebsites.net, optionally pinned on its hardcoded outdated Chrome 86 / Edg 86.0.622.38 User-Agent. This is the operator-controlled SOCKS5 relay channel turning the victim into a covert exit node. Rationale: Uses the exact BridgeHead C2 FQDN (smartconnect.azurewebsites.net) and its hardcoded, stale User-Agent (Chrome/86.0.4240.75 ... Edg/86.0.622.38) from the verbatim GET /connect request block. The stale UA on an azurewebsites host is a strong secondary discriminator when proxy telemetry is available. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/mirage-kitten-new-tools/120811/ • https://www.techtimes.com/articles/321943/20260729/iran-deploys-nightledger-backdoor-websocket-relays-across-six-nations.htm

  • Blue-team detonation of the sample
  • Security appliance URL categorization touching the host

AppVShNotify.exe making outbound public network connections (NightLedger host beaconing) AICommand & Control Medium
Hunts the NightLedger execution host: the legitimate App-V AppVShNotify.exe binary is hollowed by the SspiCli.dll side-load and never legitimately talks to the internet. Any public-IP connection initiated by AppVShNotify.exe is a high-fidelity implant signal, independent of the C2 domain rotating. Rationale: AppVShNotify.exe is the specific signed binary the article names as the side-load target; it is a local App-V notification helper with no legitimate need for outbound/internet sockets, so process-scoped egress is a resilient detection that survives C2-domain rotation. Complements the domain IOC (UC2) and the image-load anomaly (UC1) with a process→network pivot. Cross-checked against: • https://securelist.com/mirage-kitten-new-tools/120811/ • https://research.checkpoint.com/2026/fast-and-furious-nimbus-manticore-operations-during-the-iranian-conflict/
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1574.001
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic
Expected false positives · estimated rate: low — model-assessed, tune to your environment
  • Genuine App-V streaming/publishing where AppVShNotify.exe contacts an internal App-V management server (should be private IP, excluded by RemoteIPType==Public)
  • EDR/telemetry attributing a child socket to the App-V helper during a legit virtualized-app launch
  • Enterprise App-V infrastructure using a cloud-hosted publishing endpoint (rare; validate the destination)
  • Proxy-forced connections attributed to the process
Suppression filter
| where not (RemoteIPType != "Public")
Response runbook
1) Triage: capture the loaded module list of the flagged AppVShNotify.exe PID and confirm a non-System32 SspiCli.dll is present; correlate the destination IP with realhealthshop.com / tjconsultingservices.com. 2) Contain: isolate the host and terminate the process; quarantine the rogue DLL. 3) Hunt: pivot on the destination IP across the estate, and check for the BridgeHead tunneler and NetSetup.log collection on the same host.

Beaconing — periodic outbound to small set of destinations Command & Control Medium
C2 channel detection via inter-beacon-time stddev / fan-out to single dest.
ATT&CKT1071.001 T1071.004
Data sourcesNetwork_Traffic.All_Traffic DeviceNetworkEvents
DeviceNetworkEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(1d)
| where RemoteIPType == "Public" and ActionType == "ConnectionSuccess"
| project DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort, Timestamp
| sort by DeviceName asc, RemoteIP asc, RemotePort asc, Timestamp asc
| extend prev_dev = prev(DeviceName, 1), prev_ip = prev(RemoteIP, 1),
         prev_port = prev(RemotePort, 1), prev_ts = prev(Timestamp, 1)
| where DeviceName == prev_dev and RemoteIP == prev_ip and RemotePort == prev_port
| extend delta_sec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, prev_ts)
| summarize conn_count = count(), avg_delta = avg(delta_sec), stdev_delta = stdev(delta_sec)
    by DeviceName, RemoteIP, RemotePort
| where conn_count > 30 and avg_delta between (30.0 .. 600.0) and stdev_delta < 5.0
| order by conn_count desc
ImNetworkSession
| where TimeGenerated > ago(2h)
| where DvcAction != "Block"
| where ipv4_is_private(DstIpAddr) == false
| summarize ConnCount = count(),
            DistinctMinutes = dcount(bin(TimeGenerated, 1m))
            by DvcHostname, DstIpAddr, DstPortNumber
| where ConnCount > 50 and DistinctMinutes > 30   // sustained, not bursty
| order by ConnCount desc

Phishing-link click correlated to endpoint execution Delivery High
User received an external email containing a URL, clicked it, and within 60 seconds the recipient's endpoint spawned a scripting interpreter or network downloader (PowerShell / cmd / mshta / curl / certutil / bitsadmin). This is the high-fidelity shape — a raw "user clicked an emailed URL" query is too noisy to alert on because every Microsoft 365 tenant generates thousands of legitimate clicks per day. The exec-correlation step suppresses the noise to roughly the rate at which a click actually drives onto-host execution, which is the actionable signal. Pair with UC_PHISH_LINK_BYPASS (Safe Links override) and UC_PHISH_LINK_NEWDOMAIN for orthogonal high-fidelity variants.
ATT&CKT1566.002 T1204.001 T1059.001
Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Web Endpoint.Processes EmailEvents EmailUrlInfo UrlClickEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s.
// Far higher fidelity than "every clicked URL" — most legitimate clicks
// never spawn a non-browser child process, so the join eliminates the
// 99% of noise that makes a raw click query unactionable.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectClicks = UrlClickEvents
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | where ActionType in ("ClickAllowed","ClickedThrough")
    | join kind=inner (
        EmailEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
        | where DeliveryAction == "Delivered"
        | where EmailDirection == "Inbound"
        | project NetworkMessageId, Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain,
                  RecipientEmailAddress, EmailTimestamp = Timestamp
      ) on NetworkMessageId
    | join kind=leftouter (
        EmailUrlInfo | project NetworkMessageId, Url, UrlDomain
      ) on NetworkMessageId, Url
    | project ClickTime = Timestamp, AccountUpn, IPAddress, Url, UrlDomain,
              Subject, SenderFromAddress, SenderFromDomain, RecipientEmailAddress,
              ActionType;
// Correlate to a non-browser child process spawned within 60 seconds on
// the recipient's device.
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                         "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                        "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                        "bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe","curl.exe","wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectClicks on $left.AccountName == $right.AccountUpn
| where Timestamp between (ClickTime .. ClickTime + 60s)
| project ClickTime, ProcessTime = Timestamp,
          DelaySec = datetime_diff('second', Timestamp, ClickTime),
          DeviceName, AccountName, RecipientEmailAddress, SenderFromAddress,
          Subject, Url, UrlDomain, ActionType,
          FileName, ProcessCommandLine, InitiatingProcessFileName
| order by ClickTime desc
// UrlClickEvents has no clean Sentinel-native equivalent. The detection
// pivots to: Office Send→Receive proximity to SecurityEvent 4688 of a
// non-browser child process. Lower fidelity than the Defender version.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let SuspectMailUsers = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation =~ "Receive"
    | summarize Subject = any(Subject), Sender = any(Sender)
                by MailboxOwnerUPN, MessageId, EmailTime = bin(TimeGenerated, 1m);
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName has_any ("\chrome.exe","\msedge.exe",
                                     "\firefox.exe","\outlook.exe","\brave.exe")
| where NewProcessName has_any ("\powershell.exe","\pwsh.exe","\cmd.exe",
                                  "\mshta.exe","\rundll32.exe","\regsvr32.exe",
                                  "\wscript.exe","\cscript.exe","\bitsadmin.exe",
                                  "\certutil.exe","\curl.exe","\wget.exe")
| join kind=inner SuspectMailUsers on $left.Account == $right.MailboxOwnerUPN
| where TimeGenerated between (EmailTime .. EmailTime + 60s)
| project TimeGenerated, EmailTime, Computer, Account, ParentProcessName,
          NewProcessName, CommandLine, Sender, Subject
| order by TimeGenerated desc
WEB
Toggle if your env has no CIM data-model acceleration.
``` Phishing-link click that drives endpoint execution within 60s ```
| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS click_time
    from datamodel=Web
    where Web.action="allowed"
    by Web.src, Web.user, Web.dest, Web.url
| `drop_dm_object_name(Web)`
| rename user AS recipient, dest AS clicked_domain, url AS clicked_url
| join type=inner recipient
    [| tstats `summariesonly` count
         from datamodel=Email.All_Email
         where All_Email.action="delivered" AND All_Email.url!="-"
         by All_Email.recipient, All_Email.src_user, All_Email.url, All_Email.subject
     | `drop_dm_object_name(All_Email)`
     | rex field=url "https?://(?<email_domain>[^/]+)"
     | rename recipient AS recipient]
| join type=inner src
    [| tstats `summariesonly` earliest(_time) AS exec_time
         values(Processes.process) AS exec_cmd, values(Processes.process_name) AS exec_proc
         from datamodel=Endpoint.Processes
         where Processes.parent_process_name IN ("chrome.exe","msedge.exe","firefox.exe",
                                                   "outlook.exe","brave.exe","arc.exe")
           AND Processes.process_name IN ("powershell.exe","pwsh.exe","cmd.exe","mshta.exe",
                                            "rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe","wscript.exe",
                                            "cscript.exe","bitsadmin.exe","certutil.exe",
                                            "curl.exe","wget.exe")
         by Processes.dest, Processes.user
     | `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
     | rename dest AS src]
| eval delta_sec = exec_time - click_time
| where delta_sec >= 0 AND delta_sec <= 60
| table click_time, exec_time, delta_sec, recipient, src, src_user, subject,
        clicked_domain, clicked_url, exec_proc, exec_cmd
| sort - click_time

Data sourcesEmail.All_Email Endpoint.Processes EmailAttachmentInfo DeviceProcessEvents
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MalAttachments = EmailAttachmentInfo
    | where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where AccountName !endswith "$"
    | project NetworkMessageId, RecipientEmailAddress,
              AttachmentFileName = FileName, AttachmentSHA256 = SHA256;
DeviceProcessEvents
| where Timestamp > ago(LookbackDays)
| where InitiatingProcessFileName in~ ("OUTLOOK.EXE","winword.exe","excel.exe","powerpnt.exe")
| where FileName in~ ("cmd.exe","powershell.exe","wscript.exe","cscript.exe",
                      "mshta.exe","rundll32.exe","regsvr32.exe")
| join kind=inner MalAttachments on $left.AccountUpn == $right.RecipientEmailAddress
| project Timestamp, DeviceName, AccountName, FileName, ProcessCommandLine,
          InitiatingProcessFileName, AttachmentFileName, AttachmentSHA256
// Sentinel doesn't have an attachment-hash table. Best approximation:
// OfficeActivity Send/Receive activity + correlate to host process spawn.
let LookbackDays = 7d;
let MailWithAttach = OfficeActivity
    | where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
    | where OfficeWorkload == "Exchange"
    | where Operation in~ ("Send","Receive")
    | where isnotempty(MessageId)
    | project EmailTime = TimeGenerated, MailboxOwnerUPN, Sender,
              Recipients, Subject, MessageId;
SecurityEvent
| where TimeGenerated > ago(LookbackDays)
| where EventID == 4688
| where ParentProcessName endswith "OUTLOOK.EXE"
| where NewProcessName endswith "\cmd.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\powershell.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\wscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\cscript.exe"
   or NewProcessName endswith "\mshta.exe"
| project TimeGenerated, Computer, Account, NewProcessName, CommandLine,
          ParentProcessName
| order by TimeGenerated desc

File hash IOCs — endpoint file/process match Installation High
Match SHA256/SHA1/MD5 named in the article against EDR file/process telemetry.
ATT&CKT1027
Data sourcesEndpoint.Filesystem Endpoint.Processes DeviceFileEvents DeviceProcessEvents
// Pre-filter each table BEFORE the union — the engine cannot push
// predicates across `union`, and DeviceFileEvents lacks ProcessCommandLine
// (Initiating only) so column-projection has to differ per side.
union
    ( DeviceFileEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where InitiatingProcessAccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or SHA1 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or MD5 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = InitiatingProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceFileEvents" ),
    ( DeviceProcessEvents
        | where Timestamp > ago(7d)
        | where AccountName !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or SHA1 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac") or MD5 in~ ("c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac")
        | project Timestamp, DeviceName, ActionType, FileName, FolderPath, SHA256,
                  Cmd = ProcessCommandLine, Source = "DeviceProcessEvents" )
| order by Timestamp desc

// Pre-filter both ASIM tables before the union — same pattern as the
// Defender version so the engine can push hash predicates per-side.
let BadHashes = dynamic(["c832ecd135781b11f59e3fffb3d2b6ac"]);
union
    ( ImFileEvent
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where TargetFileSHA256 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileSHA1 in~ (BadHashes)
           or TargetFileMD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType,
                  TargetFileName, TargetFilePath, TargetFileSHA256,
                  Cmd = ActingProcessName, Source = "ImFileEvent" ),
    ( ImProcessCreate
        | where TimeGenerated > ago(7d)
        | where ActorUsername !endswith "$"
        | where SHA256 in~ (BadHashes) or SHA1 in~ (BadHashes) or MD5 in~ (BadHashes)
        | project TimeGenerated, DvcHostname, EventType = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileName = TargetProcessName, TargetFilePath = TargetProcessName,
                  TargetFileSHA256 = SHA256,
                  Cmd = TargetProcessCommandLine, Source = "ImProcessCreate" )
| order by TimeGenerated desc

| `drop_dm_object_name(Processes)`
| `security_content_ctime(firstTime)`
| append [
| tstats summariesonly=false count
    from datamodel=Endpoint.Filesystem
    where Filesystem.action IN ("created","modified")
      AND (Filesystem.file_path="*C:\Windows\debug\NetSetup.log*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*C:\Windows\debug\*" OR Filesystem.file_path="*%LocalAppData%\Microsoft\VisualStudio*" OR Filesystem.file_name IN ("sspicli.dll","appvshnotify.exe","rpcrt4.dll","unbcl.dll","libwinpthread-1.dll","advapi32.dll","iphlpapi.dll"))
    by Filesystem.dest, Filesystem.user, Filesystem.process_name,
       Filesystem.file_path, Filesystem.file_name
| `drop_dm_object_name(Filesystem)`
]

title: Trusted Vendor Binary Launching Unusual Children
id: 7c8b2e10-4d4f-4a8c-9c1f-bf6e2c4a8d09
status: experimental
description: |
    A signed vendor binary (in the article's named-vendors list)
    spawning script-host or LOLBin children. Strongest signal when
    paired with a recent CVE / supply-chain advisory naming the same
    vendor.
references:
    - https://attack.mitre.org/techniques/T1195/
author: Clankerusecase
date: 2026/05/02
tags:
    - attack.t1195
    - attack.initial-access
logsource:
    category: process_creation
    product: windows
detection:
    selection_child:
        Image|endswith:
            - '\powershell.exe'
            - '\pwsh.exe'
            - '\cmd.exe'
            - '\rundll32.exe'
            - '\regsvr32.exe'
            - '\mshta.exe'
            - '\wscript.exe'
            - '\cscript.exe'
            - '\wmic.exe'
            - '\bitsadmin.exe'
    selection_parent_vendor:
        ParentImage|contains:
            - '\Program Files\'
            - '\Program Files (x86)\'
    condition: selection_child and selection_parent_vendor
falsepositives:
    - Many — installers and updaters legitimately spawn children. Pin
      the parent path to specific vendor binaries observed in the
      article before alerting.
level: medium

Compromised PyPI Package: mrmustard 0.7.4 Steals SSH, Cloud, and Kubernetes Credentials

StepSecurity
2026-07-25 12 use cases 4 techniques 6 kill-chain phases detected

mrmustard 0.7.4 on PyPI was a credential stealer that ran on import, exfiltrating SSH keys, AWS, and Kubernetes credentials. Here is the analysis, IOCs, and how to respond.

ATT&CKT1027 T1190 T1195.002 T1555.003
Domainsmetrics.femboy.energy femboy.energy
SHA2560404f8590fdaef95… 81f0d1291a975d01…
